That used to settle me. But I haven’t touched them. I look at them and I know I should care, I know that’s something I used to love, but there’s nothing there except this feeling that it would take more out of me than I have.\n\nAnd choir planning at church… I’ve skipped that too. Not because I don’t believe it matters, but because I just can’t seem to make myself engage with it. Same with chess. I used to enjoy sitting down to a game, thinking a few moves ahead, getting absorbed in it. Now even that feels flat, like someone took the color out of it.\n\nEven fishing—fishing, of all things—sounds like more effort than it’s worth. And that is not like me at all. There was a time when just the thought of being by the water would’ve lifted me a little. Now I think about getting the gear together, driving out, casting a line, and I just feel tired before I even start. So yes… I don’t seem to take pleasure in much of anything I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t really been keeping track of it in that way, like separating tiredness from everything else.\n\nThere are days when I feel worn down, yes, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve specifically noticed it happening even when I haven’t done anything demanding. I suppose it’s hard for me to tell, because lately even ordinary things can feel like they take more out of me than they should. But whether that’s “less energy” in the way you mean, I’m not completely sure.\n\nI can say I don’t feel like myself, physically or mentally, but I don’t want to overstate something if I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any worsening in your memory, like forgetting things more often or having trouble recalling recent events, even things that happened just a few minutes ago?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s actually part of why I finally came in. It’s not just that I’m a little forgetful in the ordinary way, like walking into a room and wondering why I’m there. It feels more like my mind won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something basic, something I should be able to do almost without thinking—and the numbers and words just… scatter on me. I’ll have the thought, and then by the time I go to put it down, it’s gone or it’s all jumbled. I find myself staring at the page, trying to gather it back, and it’s frightening because these are not complicated tasks for me. Or they didn’t used to be.\n\nIt’s like there’s a looseness in my mind, if that makes sense. I reach for something recent, something I was just holding a few minutes ago, and it slips away before I can get it out. And then I get stuck there, trying to remember what I meant to write, what number I was working with, what the next sentence was supposed to be. It makes me feel embarrassed and honestly kind of shaken.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying asleep or do you wake up very early and can't get back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I sleep… but I haven’t paid close enough attention to say whether I’m waking up very early and can’t get back to sleep, or whether I’m just aware of the night more than I used to be.\n\nThere are times I’m in bed and I don’t feel rested, but I don’t know that I can point to a pattern like, “Yes, I wake at 4 a.m. every morning,” or anything that clear. I don’t want to say yes just because everything feels off lately. I’d probably need to watch it more carefully to answer you honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you feel restless and can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have noticed that. More the slowing than the restless part, I think. I don’t feel like I can’t sit still so much as I feel like everything in me has been turned down, or thickened somehow.\n\nEven getting out of a chair takes longer than it should. I’ll know I need to stand up, and there’s this pause before I actually move, like my body hasn’t received the message yet. And then when I do move, it feels heavy and delayed, as if I’m moving through wet cement. That sounds dramatic, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nMy speech too… I hear myself taking longer to answer questions. Someone asks me something simple, and I know there’s supposed to be an answer, but it comes slowly. My tongue feels slow. My words come out with gaps in between, and I can feel people waiting. It makes me self-conscious, because I don’t think I used to be like this.\n\nSo yes, I’d say everything has slowed down — my body, my voice, even the act of starting to do something. It’s not just laziness or taking my time. It feels like there’s resistance in every little movement.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these problems with your memory and thinking? Did they come on suddenly, or have they been getting worse slowly over time?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks now, I think. I can almost mark it from when my older sister died. Before that, I don’t want to say I was perfect, I mean I’m 67, I know people forget little things, but this particular fogginess, the trouble holding onto a thought, that really seemed to start after she passed.\n\nIt didn’t feel like one morning I woke up and suddenly couldn’t think. It was more like… after her death, in the days that followed, I began noticing I wasn’t tracking things the same way. And then over these three weeks it’s felt more present, more obvious to me. Like at first I thought maybe I was just distracted, or overwhelmed, but it hasn’t lifted. It’s been hanging around since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when your memory and thinking difficulties are worse, even if your mood feels a little better, or do they seem to be linked together?\n\nPatient: They seem linked, mostly. When I feel a little lighter, my thinking is maybe a little less stuck, but it doesn’t just snap back. That’s part of why I came in, honestly — because my mind just will not hold onto things anymore.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to be able to do almost without thinking, and suddenly the numbers and words scatter on me. I’ll have the point in my head, and then when I look at the paper or the screen, it’s gone, or it’s in pieces. I’ll reread the same line over and over and still not be sure what I meant to say next.\n\nSo yes, it does seem worse when my mood is worse, but even on a slightly better moment, the thinking trouble is still there enough that I notice it. It makes ordinary things feel much harder than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt down, sad, or hopeless most of the time, and lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Most days I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before I’ve even opened my eyes properly — this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. Not exactly crying every second, but just this deep sadness, this emptiness sitting there.\n\nAnd even when my wife is being kind to me, talking gently or trying to make things feel normal, I still feel far away from her. It’s like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well and I can hear her voice coming down from above, but I can’t really reach it. I know she’s there, I know she cares, but it doesn’t seem to get through to the part of me that feels so low.\n\nSo yes, it’s been most of the time. It’s not just a bad afternoon or a mood that passes. It feels like I’m living under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in most activities, even those you used to find meaningful or enjoyable, like being with family, helping in the workshop, or anything else that used to matter to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, or close to it. That’s one of the things that scares me, because it doesn’t feel like ordinary tiredness or just being busy.\n\nI have wood pieces sitting on my bench right now — little pieces I’d already started shaping — and normally I’d keep going back to them, even if it was just for ten minutes. I’d sand an edge, look at the grain, think about what it might become. But I haven’t touched them. I walk past the bench and I know they’re there, and it’s like they belong to someone else.\n\nAnd I skipped the choir planning, which is… not really like me. I usually care about getting those things in order, making sure people know what’s happening. Same with chess. I didn’t go, and I didn’t even feel the usual pull to go. It was more like, “What’s the point of trying to sit there and keep up?”\n\nEven fishing sounds like too much effort now. That’s hard to admit, because fishing has always been one of those things that could settle me. But lately, just thinking about gathering the gear, getting out there, sitting by the water… it all feels heavier than it should. More effort than it’s worth. And that is not like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that for at least two weeks in a row, you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, and completely lost interest in most activities, even those you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would say that. It’s been most days, and not just for a few hours here and there. I wake up with it, really — before anything has even happened, there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already weighing on me.\n\nAnd even when my wife is being kind, talking gently to me, trying to reach me, I still feel so far away. The best way I can explain it is like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. I can hear her up above, I know she’s there, I know she means well, but it doesn’t really get through. I still feel stuck down there.\n\nSo yes, it’s been at least that long, and it’s been most of the day, nearly every day. It feels like I’m living inside that hopelessness rather than just having a bad spell.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period like this before, where you felt down or hopeless most of the time and lost interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I usually care about getting those things in order, making sure people know what’s happening. Same with chess. I didn’t go, and I didn’t even feel the usual pull to go. It was more like, “What’s the point of trying to sit there and keep up?”\n\nEven fishing sounds like too much effort now. That’s hard to admit, because fishing has always been one of those things that could settle me. But lately, just thinking about gathering the gear, getting out there, sitting by the water… it all feels heavier than it should. More effort than it’s worth. And that is not like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that for at least two weeks in a row, you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, and completely lost interest in most activities, even those you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would say that. It’s been most days, and not just for a few hours here and there. I wake up with it, really — before anything has even happened, there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is already weighing on me.\n\nAnd even when my wife is being kind, talking gently to me, trying to reach me, I still feel so far away. The best way I can explain it is like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. I can hear her up above, I know she’s there, I know she means well, but it doesn’t really get through. I still feel stuck down there.\n\nSo yes, it’s been at least that long, and it’s been most of the day, nearly every day. It feels like I’m living inside that hopelessness rather than just having a bad spell.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period like this before, where you felt down or hopeless most of the time and lost interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’ve had low periods before, like anyone has, I suppose — times when I was discouraged or worn down — but I don’t know if I’d call them the same as this.\n\nThis feels different in the way it’s sort of settled over everything. I’m trying to think back, because at my age you have plenty of stretches of life where things weren’t easy, but I can’t immediately point to another time and say, “Yes, that was just like this.” I may be missing something, or maybe I didn’t understand it that way at the time.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Not like this, I don’t think. At least not that I can clearly remember."
